=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Takahashi is about to go buy eel at a fish shop.
The town where he lives is divided into a grid of H rows and W columns. Each cell is either a road or a wall.
Let us denote the cell at the i-th row from the top (1\leq i \leq H) and the j-th column from the left (1\leq j \leq W) as cell (i,j).
Information about each cell is given by H strings S_1,S_2,\ldots,S_H, each of length W. Specifically, if the j-th character of S_i (1\leq i \leq H,1\leq j\leq W) is ., cell (i,j) is a road; if it is #, cell (i,j) is a wall.
He can repeatedly perform the following two types of actions in any order:

- Move to an adjacent cell (up, down, left, or right) that is within the town and is a road.
- Choose one of the four directions (up, down, left, or right) and perform a front kick in that direction.
  When he performs a front kick, for each of the cells at most 2 steps away in that direction from the cell he is currently in, if that cell is a wall, it becomes a road.
  If some of the cells at most 2 steps away are outside the town, a front kick can still be performed, but anything outside the town does not change.

He starts in cell (A,B), and he wants to move to the fish shop in cell (C,D).
It is guaranteed that both the cell where he starts and the cell with the fish shop are roads.
Find the minimum number of front kicks he needs in order to reach the fish shop.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W
S_1
S_2
\vdots
S_H
A B C D

Output

Print the minimum number of front kicks needed for Takahashi to reach the fish shop.

Constraints


- 1\leq H\leq 1000
- 1\leq W\leq 1000
- Each S_i is a string of length W consisting of . and #.
- 1\leq A,C\leq H
- 1\leq B,D\leq W
- (A,B)\neq (C,D)
- H, W, A, B, C, and D are integers.
- The cell where Takahashi starts and the cell with the fish shop are roads.

Sample Input 1

10 10
..........
#########.
#.......#.
#..####.#.
##....#.#.
#####.#.#.
.##.#.#.#.
###.#.#.#.
###.#.#.#.
#.....#...
1 1 7 1

Sample Output 1

1

Takahashi starts in cell (1,1).
By repeatedly moving to adjacent road cells, he can reach cell (7,4).
If he performs a front kick to the left from cell (7,4), cells (7,3) and (7,2) turn from walls to roads.
Then, by continuing to move through road cells (including those that have become roads), he can reach the fish shop in cell (7,1).
In this case, the number of front kicks performed is 1, and it is impossible to reach the fish shop without performing any front kicks, so print 1.

Sample Input 2

2 2
.#
#.
1 1 2 2

Sample Output 2

1

Takahashi starts in cell (1,1).
When he performs a front kick to the right, cell (1,2) turns from a wall to a road.
The cell two steps to the right of (1,1) is outside the town, so it does not change.
Then, he can move to cell (1,2) and then to the fish shop in cell (2,2).
In this case, the number of front kicks performed is 1, and it is impossible to reach the fish shop without performing any front kicks, so print 1.

Sample Input 3

1 3
.#.
1 1 1 3

Sample Output 3

1

When performing a front kick, it is fine if the fish shop’s cell is within the cells that could be turned into a road. Specifically, the fish shop’s cell is a road from the beginning, so it remains unchanged; particularly, the shop is not destroyed by the front kick.

Sample Input 4

20 20
####################
##...##....###...###
#.....#.....#.....##
#..#..#..#..#..#..##
#..#..#....##..#####
#.....#.....#..#####
#.....#..#..#..#..##
#..#..#.....#.....##
#..#..#....###...###
####################
####################
##..#..##...###...##
##..#..#.....#.....#
##..#..#..#..#..#..#
##..#..#..#..#..#..#
##.....#..#..#..#..#
###....#..#..#..#..#
#####..#.....#.....#
#####..##...###...##
####################
3 3 18 18

Sample Output 4

3

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Maybe we can model "broken walls" as nodes with cost and use 0-cost edges between any two cells that are both reachable? That's dynamic. But maybe shortest path with kicks can be represented by a graph where nodes are cells and edge weights:
- 0-cost between adjacent cells if both are originally road OR both are "reachable"? Not static.
- We can run Dijkstra where when we pop a cell with distance d, we mark it reachable; then we can propagate 0-cost to adjacent original roads and to adjacent cells with same distance? Wait if a wall is broken by a kick, its distance equals the number of kicks used at the moment it becomes road. If two walls broken by same kick have same distance. Movement between them is free, so they should be in same connected component of cells with distance <= d. This is like 0-1 BFS with "expanding" reachable set: from a cell with distance d, you can move through all cells that are either original roads or have distance <= d (already broken) at no extra cost. But if a wall has distance d, it is broken. If adjacent wall not broken, you can break it with one kick, giving it distance d+1, and maybe also the next cell in that direction distance d+1.

This suggests a BFS over distance levels where at each level, you can traverse all cells with distance <= current level. We need compute minimal d for each cell. This is like "0-1 BFS with dynamic connectivity" but can be solved by treating kicks as adding cells to a queue with distance+1, and then doing 0-cost flood fill through original roads and cells already assigned? Standard 0-1 BFS on grid for wall-breaking problems: dist[x][y] = min kicks to reach cell. Initially start dist 0. Use deque. For each popped cell, relax:
- If neighbor is original road and dist > d: set d, push front.
- If neighbor is wall and dist > d+1: set d+1, push back.
This works for breaking one wall at a time. For front kick breaking up to two walls in a line, we can relax both distance1 and distance2 walls with d+1. But movement through broken walls (walls with dist assigned) as 0-cost? In standard 0-1 BFS, when a wall is broken, it becomes road; but the algorithm's 0-cost moves only through original roads. However, in standard problem where each move into wall costs 1, once you break a wall, you can move through it; but the dist of that wall is d+1, and moving from it to adjacent original road costs 0? Standard 0-1 BFS with 0-cost to adjacent '.' and 1-cost to adjacent '#' actually handles this? Let's recall: If you enter a wall with cost1, then from that wall, moving to adjacent '.' should cost 0. In standard algorithm, when at wall cell with dist d+1, you relax adjacent '.' with d+1 (0-cost). So yes, it handles broken wall to original road. It does not handle moving from broken wall to adjacent wall that was broken earlier with 0 cost, because adjacent '#' would cost1. But in standard problem, if adjacent wall was broken earlier, it should be road; but dist of that wall is <= current, so moving to it should cost0. Does standard algorithm overcount? Wait Dijkstra/0-1 BFS property: If v already has minimal distance, any path through v to some target y can be accounted for when v was popped, using v's outgoing edges. Let's think in terms of shortest path in a graph where nodes are cells and edge costs represent "minimum additional kicks to go from one cell to another assuming all cells with dist <= current are roads"? Standard 0-1 BFS with static edges may still compute correct min because of optimal substructure: The dist of a cell is the min kicks to make it road/reachable. Once a cell has dist d, it is road. Any movement from it to adjacent cell:
- If adjacent cell is original road, cost 0.
- If adjacent cell is original wall, you can break it with a kick from current cell (cost1), and also break the next cell in that direction (cost1) if wall. This is exactly a 1-cost edge to adjacent wall and to next cell in same direction (distance2) because one kick from current can break both. Wait if adjacent cell is original wall and you are at current cell, you can kick in that direction, breaking adjacent and next. So from current, you can reach adjacent wall with cost1 and next cell (wall or road) with cost1. That's the plus-shape 1-cost edges. If adjacent cell is original road, cost0. This graph is static and does not require knowing if adjacent wall was already broken; if it was already broken, its dist <= current, so any path through it to further cells is already accounted by its own outgoing edges. So static graph should be correct! The earlier concern about moving between two broken walls is handled by the fact that both have same dist and their outgoing edges are processed when popped. If you need to go from one to the other to reach a target, the target would have been reached from the other directly with no more kicks. If you need to perform an action at one before going to the other, the action's effect (breaking some cell) might be needed to reach target from the other? But if target reachable from other after the action, the action is at one, not at other. Could the action at one create a path from other to target that didn't exist before? The action breaks cells in a direction from one. Those broken cells may connect to other? They are at distance 1/2 from one. To use them from other, you need move from other to one (free) then to broken cells. But if target is reachable via other -> one -> broken cells, then from one to broken cells is a kick; target's dist would be d+1. Static graph: from one, 1-cost edge to broken cells (d+1). From broken cells, maybe 0-cost to target. It doesn't need to go through other. If target is reachable only by other -> one -> broken cells -> ... -> target, then from one -> broken cells -> ... -> target is a valid path with same cost, so static graph captures it. If target is reachable by one -> broken cells -> ... -> other -> target, then other -> target is a path, so target dist <= d, contradiction if d+1 needed. Thus static graph likely correct.

Need be careful with 0-1 BFS and directed 0 edges. We'll implement:
```
from collections import deque
H,W = map(int,input().split())
S=[input().strip() for _ in range(H)]
A,B,C,D = map(int,input().split())
A-=1;B-=1;C-=1;D-=1
INF=10**9
dist=[[INF]*W for _ in range(H)]
dist[A][B]=0
dq=deque([(A,B)])
while dq:
    x,y=dq.popleft()
    d=dist[x][y]
    if x==C and y==D: break? We can break early because 0-1 BFS pops in nondecreasing distance. Yes, first pop target gives min. But if target reached with distance d, could there be same distance not processed? 0-1 BFS pops in nondecreasing order, so first pop target is min. We can break to save time. But need ensure if target is start? guaranteed not. We'll break.
    # 0-cost moves to adjacent original roads
    for dx,dy in dirs:
       nx=x+dx; ny=y+dy
       if 0<=nx<H and 0<=ny<W and S[nx][ny]=='.' and dist[nx][ny]>d:
           dist[nx][ny]=d; dq.appendleft((nx,ny))
    # 1-cost kicks to distance1 and distance2 in four directions
    for dx,dy in dirs:
       nx=x+dx; ny=y+dy
       if 0<=nx<H and 0<=ny<W and dist[nx][ny]>d+1:
           dist[nx][ny]=d+1; dq.append((nx,ny))
       nx=x+2*dx; ny=y+2*dy
       if 0<=nx<H and 0<=ny<W and dist[nx][ny]>d+1:
           dist[nx][ny]=d+1; dq.append((nx,ny))
print(dist[C][D])
```

Start O(3,2). 1 edge O->B(3,4) cost1. From B, kick up to C(2,4) cost1. From C, can we reach X(2,3)? C(2,4) adjacent X(2,3) original road! Yes 0 edge C->X. So static graph finds target via C with cost2. It doesn't need B->A. If X were not adjacent to C but adjacent to A, then maybe target reachable from A. But if C is broken, maybe C connects to X? Let's set X=(2,3) road, C=(2,4) wall. After kick up from B, C becomes road, and C is adjacent to X, so target reached. So action at B creates path to X directly. If target adjacent to A but not to C, then target reachable from A without action at B, so cost1. Thus no issue.

What if need both action at B to break C, and target adjacent to A but C doesn't connect to target; then target reachable from A with cost1, so no need action at B for target. If target requires both C and A? Since target is a single cell; if reachable from A, done. If not reachable from A, then path from C to target may not need A. So static graph likely okay.

This is essentially the property of shortest paths in graphs with "teleport" edges: If two nodes are connected by zero-cost in actual after being broken, but not in static graph, the static graph still has a path of same cost by choosing the node with smaller/equal dist as the entry point. Because the zero-cost connection is between nodes with equal dist (broken by same kick) or nodes with one already reachable. In general, any actual movement between two reachable cells with no additional kick corresponds to both cells having dist <= current. If one has smaller dist, its outgoing edges already processed. If equal, either can be used as entry. So static graph with edges representing actions from a cell is sufficient: The state is just current cell; to perform an action, you must be at a cell. The cost to be at a cell is min kicks. Movement between cells with no extra kick is not an "action" that changes cost; if a cell y is reachable with cost <= dist[x], then any continuation from y is already considered from y. If y has same cost but was reached via a different path, you can "be at y" instead of x for the continuation. If you need to be at x for an action and then y for another action, and x,y are connected free, then the action at x plus action at y can be done in either order? Actually if you need to do action at x then action at y, you can do action at x, then move to y free. Static graph: from x, action to z; from z, can it reach y? Not necessarily. But if y was reachable with same cost before action at x, then after action at x, you could have done action at y first? Wait order matters because action at x may break cells needed to reach y? But y was already reachable before action at x, so no. If y was not reachable before action at x, then action at x creates a path to y? Then static graph can capture x->z->...->y. If action at x doesn't create path to y, then y was reachable before. So no need to model x->y free. This is a common argument for 0-1 BFS with "wall break" edges.

Actual sequence consists of moves (0 cost) and kicks (1 cost). We can compress each kick and following moves. Consider after a kick, a set of cells become road: the two cells in direction. Then he may move through a connected component of roads (original plus broken). The component may include cells broken by previous kicks too. The cost to enter any cell in this component is the number of kicks performed so far. In graph, we want a path with same number of kicks to the final cell. We can induct on kicks: after t kicks, the set R_t of reachable cells (roads) is known. Claim: For every cell v in R_t, there is a graph path from start to v with cost <= t. Base t=0: original road component reachable by 0 edges, yes. Inductive step: Suppose after t kicks, all cells in R_t have graph distance <= t. Next kick is performed from some cell p in R_t. By induction, p has graph distance <= t. The kick breaks q1, q2 (distance1/2) in direction. After kick, R_{t+1} includes R_t plus q1,q2 plus any original road cells connected to q1/q2 via roads (including broken walls? Actually broken walls from previous kicks are in R_t, so connected component). Need show any new cell v in R_{t+1} has graph distance <= t+1. If v in R_t, done. If v is q1 or q2, graph edge p->v cost1, so distance <= t+1. If v is an original road cell connected to q1 or q2 through a path of cells in R_{t+1}. The path from q to v may go through broken walls from previous kicks (in R_t) and original roads. If it goes through a broken wall w in R_t, then w already has graph distance <= t. From w, can we reach v with 0-cost edges? Only if path consists of original roads? But path may include other broken walls. However, by induction, v might already be in R_t? Wait v is original road connected to q via R_{t+1}; if v was not in R_t before kick, then the path from q to v must include at least one newly broken cell q (q1/q2), and then possibly cells in R_t. The segment from q to v: q is newly broken; adjacent cells along path may be in R_t (broken walls or original roads). If adjacent cell is original road, 0 edge q->adj. If adjacent cell is broken wall w in R_t, then w has graph distance <= t. From w, can we reach v with <=? If v is original road connected to w via R_t, by induction v in R_t? Actually if v is original road and connected to w via R_t, then v would have been reachable before kick, so v in R_t. Contradiction if v not in R_t. Thus any original road v not in R_t that becomes reachable after kick must be adjacent (through original roads?) to a newly broken cell without needing to traverse other broken walls? Let's analyze.

R_t is the set of all cells reachable before kick. It is closed under movement through roads (original and broken). If an original road v is not in R_t, then no path from R_t to v using roads exists before kick. After kick, q1/q2 are added. If v becomes connected to R_t, the path from q to v must start with q (new), then go through cells. The next cell after q could be:
- original road r: if r not in R_t, then r is connected to v via original roads? Since r is original road and adjacent to q. If r not in R_t, then after kick, r becomes reachable. From r, can move through original roads to v. So graph has 0 edge q->r (because r original road), and then 0 edges through original roads to v. Thus graph distance <= t+1.
- broken wall w in R_t: then w is in R_t. From w, can move through R_t to maybe original roads. If v is connected via w and other R_t cells, then v would be in R_t? Wait v is original road; if there is a path from w to v through R_t (which may include broken walls and original roads), then v is reachable before kick, contradiction. So v cannot be connected to w through R_t unless v in R_t. Thus the path from q to v cannot go through a broken wall in R_t before reaching v, because then v would have been reachable before. Therefore the path from q to v consists entirely of newly broken q and original roads not in R_t? Actually after kick, only q1/q2 are newly broken; no other walls become road. So any path from q to v can only use q1/q2 as non-original-road cells, and then original roads. Since q1 and q2 are adjacent to each other; if path goes q1->q2, q2 is also newly broken. Then from q2 to original roads. Graph has edge p->q1 and p->q2 cost1, and 0 edges from q1/q2 to adjacent original roads. So v reachable with cost t+1. If path goes q1 -> original road r -> ... -> v, graph 0 edges. If path goes q1 -> q2 -> original road r, graph can use p->q2 cost1 then 0 to r. So yes. This induction proves static graph is correct! Nice.

Important: The induction relies on R_t being closed under roads, and if an original road v becomes reachable after adding q's, there is a path from q to v that doesn't need other broken walls in R_t because otherwise v would have been reachable before. Thus static graph with 0 edges from any cell to adjacent original roads and 1 edges to q1/q2 is sufficient. Great.

Sample 1: start can reach (7,4) road. From (7,4), kick left: distance1 (7,3) wall d+1, distance2 (7,2) wall d+1. From (7,2) 0 edge to target (7,1) road. Answer1. Good.

What about a scenario where distance1 is wall and distance2 is road, but distance1 wall is not adjacent to distance2 road? It is adjacent by definition. 0 edge from wall to road. Good.

Thus 1 edges only to walls is correct and more efficient. But wait, what about a wall at distance2 where distance1 is also wall; direct edge to distance2 wall cost1. If we only add distance2 wall, okay. If distance1 wall is not added? We add both.

Now, is there any need to add 1-cost edge to a wall at distance2 if distance1 is outside? no.

Let's think about 0-cost edges: From any cell to adjacent original road. If current cell is original wall and reachable, 0 edge to adjacent original road. We implement by checking S[nx][ny]=='.'. Good.

But what about moving from original road to adjacent original wall that was already broken (dist <= current)? Static graph doesn't have 0 edge, but as argued not needed. However, in implementation, when popping an original road cell x with dist d, and neighbor y is original wall with dist d (already broken by same kick? Could happen if y was broken by a kick from some other cell with same cost). We do not relax y because y is wall and 1-cost would set d+1 if not visited, but dist[y]=d so no update. That's fine. But if we need to move from x to y to reach target, target would have been reached from y when y was popped. If y hasn't been popped yet but has dist d, it's in deque. It will be popped and relax target. So no issue.

Let's consider a path where original road x is reached with dist d, and adjacent wall y was broken earlier with dist d-1 (so y popped earlier). From x, moving to y is free but static graph would not relax y (already lower). Target beyond y already relaxed. Fine.

Now, is 0-1 BFS with directed 0 edges from walls to roads but not roads to walls (if wall already broken) still yields correct distances? We argued yes because any path that would go road->broken wall can be replaced by starting at broken wall with <= cost. But what if the road x is reached with cost d, and broken wall y has dist d (same), and target z is reachable from y with cost d, so z already has dist <= d. If z is not discovered yet because y not popped, it will be when y popped. So no need to traverse x->y. If target z requires doing an action at x before going to y? If action at x breaks something, then from that something maybe to z via y. But if z via y is reachable without action at x, then z dist <= d. If action at x is needed to reach z from y, then z is reachable from the broken something, not via y necessarily. So okay.

Let's try to prove static graph distances equal actual min kicks more rigorously:
Let G be graph with nodes cells, edges:
- For any cell u and adjacent original road v, edge u->v weight 0.
- For any cell u and direction dir, for k=1,2, if u+k*dir inside and is original wall, edge u->(u+k*dir) weight 1.
We claim dist_G(start,v) = min kicks to make v reachable (road) and stand there.

Upper bound: Any edge corresponds to a valid action/move from a road cell. If u is road/reachable, 0 edge to adjacent original road is valid. 1 edge to wall at distance k: perform front kick in direction; if k=1, wall becomes road; if k=2, both distance1 and distance2 walls? Wait if distance1 is original road, kick breaks distance2; if distance1 is original wall, kick breaks both. In either case, the wall at distance k becomes road, and you can move to it (through distance1, which is road after kick or already road). So valid. Thus any graph path realizes a sequence with same number of kicks.

Lower bound: Take any actual sequence with K kicks that ends at v. We show by induction on number of kicks that after each kick, every cell in the reachable road component has graph distance <= number of kicks. Base: initial road component reachable by 0 edges (since 0 edges connect adjacent original roads; if component has original roads, yes). Inductive step: Suppose before a kick, all reachable cells have graph dist <= t. The kick is from some cell p in reachable component, so dist_G(p) <= t. It breaks q1 = p+dir and q2 = p+2dir (if inside and walls; if road no change). Let R be reachable component before kick, R' after kick. Any cell v in R' \ R must be connected in R' to q1 or q2 by a path whose first edge from q1/q2 goes to an original road? Let's examine: R' = R ∪ {q1 if wall, q2 if wall} plus original roads connected to these via original roads? Actually if q1/q2 are broken, they become roads; then any original road cell connected to q1/q2 through a path of original roads and the newly broken q's becomes reachable. But R already includes all original roads connected to R. So R' \ R consists of cells in the connected component (via original roads and q's) of q1/q2 that were not connected to R before. Since q1/q2 are adjacent to p (in R) and become road, they connect to R. The new component includes q1/q2 and any original roads connected to q1/q2 without passing through R? Actually if an original road r is connected to q1 via a path of original roads that does not include R, then r becomes reachable. If the path from q1 to r goes through another broken wall from previous kicks, that broken wall is in R, so then r would have been connected to R before, contradiction. Thus any new original road r is connected to q1 or q2 by a path consisting solely of original roads (except the starting q). Therefore, from q1/q2, there is a 0-cost path in G to r (because 0 edges connect adjacent original roads; from q1 (wall) to adjacent original road is 0; then original roads). Since dist_G(q1) <= t+1 via edge p->q1 (if q1 wall; if q1 road, it was in R? Actually if q1 road, then q1 in R, so not new; if q1 wall, edge exists). Similarly q2. Thus dist_G(r) <= t+1. If v is q1/q2 wall, edge exists. So all new cells have dist <= t+1. Cells in R already <= t. Thus after K kicks, v has dist <= K. Therefore min graph distance <= actual min kicks. Combined with upper bound, equality. This proof relies on 1 edge to q2 even if q1 is road? If q2 wall and q1 road, edge p->q2 weight1 exists (we add wall at distance2). If q2 wall and q1 wall, edge exists. If q2 road, not new. Good. If q1 wall, edge p->q1 exists. Good.

Thus static graph is correct.

Now, implementation details for performance.

Input H W up to 1000, S lines. A B C D 1-indexed convert.

Use `deque` from collections.

Potential issue: Python recursion none.

We can store dist as list of lists of int initialized to -1 (unvisited) instead of INF to avoid comparing to INF? Use -1 for unvisited. Then:
```
if dist[nx][ny] == -1:
    dist[nx][ny] = d
    dq.appendleft(...)
```
Since 0-1 BFS first time a node is discovered is with minimal distance? In 0-1 BFS with deque, yes, if we only set once, it's minimal? Standard 0-1 BFS can set multiple times if using >, but if using unvisited, is first discovery always minimal? For 0-1 BFS with deque, when you push front for 0 and back for 1, nodes are popped in nondecreasing distance. However, a node might be discovered with distance d+1 before a path with distance d is found? Is that possible? In Dijkstra, first time you pop is final; but first time you discover might not be final if there is a 0-edge later? Let's recall: In 0-1 BFS, if you use `if dist[v] > d+w`, you may update multiple times. If you use `if dist[v] == -1`, is it safe? I think yes for 0-1 BFS? Let's verify. Standard BFS with deque and unvisited: When you pop a node, its distance is minimal. But a node can be enqueued before its minimal distance is known? Example: start s, edges: s->a weight1, s->b weight0, b->a weight0. Process s: push a back (dist1), push b front (dist0). Pop b, relax a via 0, a already has dist1 but should be 0. If using unvisited, a was already visited with dist1, so we would not update to 0. Thus first discovery is not minimal! So need allow updates with `>` or use Dijkstra. Important.

So use `dist` initialized to INF (or large) and update if `d + w < dist[nx][ny]`. This may enqueue a node multiple times, but okay. Or use `if dist[nx][ny] > d` for 0 and `> d+1` for 1. Need push when updated. This is standard.

Could we use `-1` and still update if `dist == -1 or dist > d+w`? Yes. But if we update multiple times, need store int. Use INF.

Memory: `dist = [[INF]*W for _ in range(H)]`. INF = 10**9. When update, store int.

Time: Updating multiple times could be more than 1e6, but still each edge relaxed once per pop; a node may be popped multiple times if updated. In 0-1 BFS with `>` updates, each node can be updated multiple times but typically O(E). Worst-case? 0-1 BFS with `>` is Dijkstra on 0-1 weights; each edge can relax once per endpoint update, but a node can be updated O(degree) times? In 0-1 BFS, if using deque and `>`, each edge may cause relaxation when its source is popped. A node can be popped multiple times if its distance improves. But with 0-1 weights, number of pops can be O(E) in worst case? Actually standard 0-1 BFS with `>` is correct and O(E) because each edge relaxation can happen at most once? Let's recall: In 0-1 BFS, a vertex can be inserted multiple times, but total O(E) because each edge is relaxed when the vertex is popped; a vertex can be popped multiple times if distance decreases. Could distance decrease many times? With 0/1 weights, distances are integers; but a vertex might be updated from d+1 to d via 0-edge after being popped? Example above: a updated dist1, pushed back; then b popped, updated a to 0, push front. a popped twice. Could happen multiple times? Potentially O(E). Still okay for 1e6, 12e6 edges maybe. But to be safe, we can use a different approach to avoid multiple updates? Maybe use 0-1 BFS with `if dist[nx][ny] > d+w` and append; standard accepted for 1e6.

Could use `heapq` Dijkstra? O(E log V) too slow maybe but 12e6 log maybe too slow. 0-1 BFS is fine.

Need optimize inner loops.

Option: Use flat list for dist to improve cache and avoid list-of-lists overhead? Let's consider.

H,W up to 1000, 1e6. List of lists access `dist[x][y]` two indexing operations. Flat list `dist = [INF]*(H*W)`, index = x*W+y. This may be faster and memory lower. We'll use flat list. Grid strings list for S. For neighbor index, compute nx*W+ny. Need check bounds.

Pseudo:
```
from collections import deque
import sys
def main():
    input=sys.stdin.readline
    H,W=map(int,input().split())
    S=[input().strip() for _ in range(H)]
    A,B,C,D=map(int,input().split())
    A-=1;B-=1;C-=1;D-=1
    INF=10**9
    dist=[INF]*(H*W)
    start=A*W+B
    goal=C*W+D
    dist[start]=0
    dq=deque([start])
    dirs=((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1))
    while dq:
        v=dq.popleft()
        d=dist[v]
        if v==goal:
            break
        x=v//W; y=v-x*W  # or v%W
        # 0 moves
        if x>0:
            nv=v-W
            if S[x-1][y]=='.' and dist[nv]>d:
                dist[nv]=d; dq.appendleft(nv)
        if x+1<H:
            nv=v+W
            if S[x+1][y]=='.' and dist[nv]>d:
                dist[nv]=d; dq.appendleft(nv)
        if y>0:
            nv=v-1
            if S[x][y-1]=='.' and dist[nv]>d:
                dist[nv]=d; dq.appendleft(nv)
        if y+1<W:
            nv=v+1
            if S[x][y+1]=='.' and dist[nv]>d:
                dist[nv]=d; dq.appendleft(nv)
        # 1 kicks
        # up
        if x>=2:
            nv=v-2*W
            if S[x-2][y]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
        if x>=1:
            nv=v-W
            if S[x-1][y]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
        # down
        if x+2<H:
            nv=v+2*W
            if S[x+2][y]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
        if x+1<H:
            nv=v+W
            if S[x+1][y]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
        # left
        if y>=2:
            nv=v-2
            if S[x][y-2]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
        if y>=1:
            nv=v-1
            if S[x][y-1]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
        # right
        if y+2<W:
            nv=v+2
            if S[x][y+2]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
        if y+1<W:
            nv=v+1
            if S[x][y+1]=='#' and dist[nv]>d+1:
                dist[nv]=d+1; dq.append(nv)
    print(dist[goal])
```
But note: For 1 kicks, we add distance1 wall and distance2 wall. If distance1 is wall and distance2 is wall, both added. If distance1 is wall and distance2 is road, distance1 added; distance2 not because S != '#'. Good. If distance1 road and distance2 wall, distance2 added. If distance1 road and distance2 road, none. Good.

However, is it sufficient to add only walls for 1-cost edges? What about reaching a wall at distance2 when distance1 is wall, but distance1 wall is outside? no. Good.

But wait: What if distance1 is wall and distance2 is wall, and we add distance2 wall cost1. But to move from current cell to distance2, you need distance1 to be road after kick. It is. Good.

What if distance1 is wall and distance2 is wall, but distance1 is not inside? impossible.

If distance1 is a wall and distance2 is a road, distance1 becomes road. If distance1 is a road and distance2 is a wall, distance2 becomes road. If both roads, no change. So adding only walls at distance1/2 is exactly the cells that become road. But what about moving to a road cell at distance2 when distance1 is wall? We don't add road cell, but we add distance1 wall, then 0 edge to road. Good. What about moving to a road cell at distance1? 0 edge. Good.

Now, is there any case where a kick breaks a wall at distance2 but distance1 is wall, and distance1 wall is not added because it is already road? If it's wall, added. If it's already road, no need. Good.

Let's test with a scenario: current cell at (0,0), direction right, distance1 wall, distance2 wall. We add (0,1) and (0,2). From (0,2) 0 edge to adjacent original roads. Good.

Now, what about a kick from a wall cell that was broken but not yet "visited" because dist updated but not popped? In BFS, we only process popped nodes. Fine.

Potential issue with early break: If we break when popping goal, but goal might be updated later with smaller distance? In 0-1 BFS with `>` updates, first pop of a node is guaranteed minimal? Let's confirm with 0-1 BFS using deque and allowing updates. Standard algorithm: pop left, relax 0 edges push front, 1 edges push back. It maintains deque sorted by distance (nondecreasing). When a node is popped, its distance is minimal because all nodes with smaller distance have been popped and all nodes with same distance are ahead? But if a node was enqueued with a larger distance earlier, and later updated to smaller distance, it may appear twice. The first time it is popped is when the smaller distance entry reaches front? Actually if updated to smaller distance, it is pushed front, so it will be popped before the larger distance entry. Thus first pop is minimal. So early break safe. Need ensure we don't break on an old larger-distance entry before smaller? Since smaller pushed front, it will be popped first. Good.

Let's test the earlier example where first discovery not minimal: s->a weight1, s->b weight0, b->a weight0. Process s: a dist1 append back, b dist0 append front. Deque [b,a]. Pop b, update a dist0 appendleft. Deque [a,a]. Pop a dist0. First pop of a is dist0. Good. So early break safe.

Let's write optimized code:
```
def main():
    import sys
    from collections import deque
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    H,W = map(int,input().split())
    S = [input().strip() for _ in range(H)]
    A,B,C,D = map(int,input().split())
    A-=1; B-=1; C-=1; D-=1
    start = A*W+B
    goal = C*W+D
    INF = 10**9
    dist = [INF]*(H*W)
    dist[start]=0
    dq=deque([start])
    while dq:
        v=dq.popleft()
        d=dist[v]
        if v==goal:
            break
        x = v // W
        y = v - x*W
        # 0-cost
        if x > 0:
            nv = v - W
            if S[x-1][y] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)
        if x + 1 < H:
            nv = v + W
            if S[x+1][y] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)
        if y > 0:
            nv = v - 1
            if S[x][y-1] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)
        if y + 1 < W:
            nv = v + 1
            if S[x][y+1] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)
        nd = d + 1
        # up
        if x >= 2:
            nv = v - 2*W
            if S[x-2][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if x >= 1:
            nv = v - W
            if S[x-1][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        # down
        if x + 2 < H:
            nv = v + 2*W
            if S[x+2][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if x + 1 < H:
            nv = v + W
            if S[x+1][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        # left
        if y >= 2:
            nv = v - 2
            if S[x][y-2] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if y >= 1:
            nv = v - 1
            if S[x][y-1] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        # right
        if y + 2 < W:
            nv = v + 2
            if S[x][y+2] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if y + 1 < W:
            nv = v + 1
            if S[x][y+1] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
    print(dist[goal])
```
This adds distance1 and distance2 walls separately. But note: For a kick, if distance1 is wall and distance2 is wall, both are broken. We add both with cost d+1. If distance1 is wall and distance2 is wall, and distance1 is already visited with d (same), distance2 maybe not. Good.

But wait: What if distance1 is wall and distance2 is wall, and we add distance2 wall with cost d+1. To move from current to distance2, you pass through distance1. But if distance1 is not broken? It is broken by same kick. Good. However, our graph edge current->distance2 cost1 does not require distance1 to be road in static graph; but the action itself makes it road. So valid.

Now, is there any need to add 1-cost edge to distance2 wall if distance1 is outside? no.

Let's test with a case where distance1 is wall and distance2 is wall, but distance1 is at boundary? no.

Now, let's think about 0-cost edges from a wall cell to adjacent original road. In code, when popping a wall cell (dist set), we check neighbors if original road and update. Good.

What about 0-cost edges from a wall cell to adjacent original road that is also a wall? no.

Now, could there be a path where after a kick, you move from distance2 wall to distance1 wall (both broken) and then to a road adjacent to distance1; static graph uses direct edge to distance1 wall cost1 instead. If you also need to kick from distance2, maybe static graph can use distance2 edge and then from broken cells reach road? We argued okay. Let's test a concrete potential counterexample to be safe.

Construct grid where start O, first kick right breaks A (dist1), B (dist2). Need to kick up from B to break C, then move to road X adjacent to A. We saw if C adjacent to X, static path O->B->C->X. If C not adjacent to X, then X reachable from A without C, so cost1. Let's make X not reachable from A without C? But X adjacent to A, so reachable from A directly. Thus if target X, no need C. If target is beyond X and requires C? If C is needed to reach beyond X, then from C to beyond X path exists, static captures. If target requires both C and X? A path can go O->B->C->...->target; no need X. If target is reachable only by going C -> B -> A -> X? Then X reachable from A, so target reachable from A? Wait target is X, yes. If target is beyond X, path C->B->A->X->target; but X->target is a path from A? A->X->target, so target reachable from A without C. So no.

What if target is beyond X but X->target requires C? Contradiction if C is not on path X->target. If C is on path X->target, then C->...->target exists. So static captures.

Thus okay.

Now, let's consider if 1 edges should be undirected? Kick direction from current cell; edges are directed from current to target. But 0-1 BFS processes directed edges. Are there cases where you need to kick "back" to a cell you came from? You can always kick from any road cell; if you are at target cell, you can kick back. Our graph adds edges from every popped cell in all four directions, so directed but effectively from each cell to neighbors; not necessarily symmetric because if u->v wall cost1, v->u maybe if u is original road? If u is original road, v->u cost0 (if v wall reachable) or 0 if u road. If u is wall, v->u wall cost1. So directed. But BFS follows directed edges from current to next; since we process all directions from every cell, any valid kick from current is included. Movement from current to adjacent original road included. Movement from original road to adjacent broken wall not included, but as argued not needed. So directed is fine.

Now, let's think about "road" cells that are originally '.' but may be destroyed by front kick? Statement says if fish shop cell is road, remains unchanged; front kick only turns walls into roads, never destroys roads. So original roads remain roads. Good.

Let's test with a small brute force mentally to ensure no overcount. Suppose grid 1x5: S = ".#.#.", start 1, target 5. Actual: start at1. Kick right breaks cell2 wall and cell3 road? cell3 '.' road. This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. More generally, the graph of reachable cells with cost <= t is connected; static graph distances capture the minimum cost to reach each cell, and the first time a cell is reached with minimal cost, all continuations are considered. This is exactly Dijkstra optimality: If actual path reaches cell y with cost K, then dist_G(y) <= K by induction. We already proved lower bound using induction on kicks, not relying on movement between broken walls. This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. More generally, the graph of reachable cells with cost <= t is connected; static graph distances capture the minimum cost to reach each cell, and the first time a cell is reached with minimal cost, all continuations are considered. But e is broken by w-action. After e broken, target reachable from e (because e connects to target). Then target dist <= d+2 from e, no need c. Thus no need traverse e->c. So static graph okay.

This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. More generally, the graph of reachable cells with cost <= t is connected; static graph distances capture the minimum cost to reach each cell, and the first time a cell is reached with minimal cost, all continuations are considered. This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. More generally, the graph of reachable cells with cost <= t is connected; static graph distances capture the minimum cost to reach each cell, and the first time a cell is reached with minimal cost, all continuations are considered. But e is broken by w-action. After e broken, target reachable from e (because e connects to target). Then target dist <= d+2 from e, no need c. Thus no need traverse e->c. So static graph okay.

This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. More generally, the graph of reachable cells with cost <= t is connected; static graph distances capture the minimum cost to reach each cell, and the first time a cell is reached with minimal cost, all continuations are considered. This suggests any path that uses movement between broken walls can be shortcut: if you need to go from A to B (both broken) to reach target, and B was broken before/at same cost, then target reachable from B without A, or if A needed to break B, then target reachable from A's broken cell without B. Then r1->r2 0 because both original roads. Good. If q1 is a wall, we allow 0 edge to adjacent original road. Good.

Now, let's consider if q1 is a wall and r1 is original road, but q1 is not in R_t? It is newly broken, so in R'. Static graph edge p->q1 cost1, then q1->r1 0. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is adjacent to q2, static graph can use p->q2 cost1, then q2->r 0. Good.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and an original road r is adjacent to q1 but q1 is not added because q1 is wall? It is added. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is adjacent to both q1 and q2? possible, but no issue.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then original roads, static graph p->q2->r. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then a previously broken wall w, r would be in R_t, so not new. Good.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then another newly broken? There are only q1/q2 new. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then q1 again? cycle, no.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then original roads, static graph p->q2->r. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then original roads, but q2 is not added because q2 is wall? It is added. Good.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then original roads, but q2 is outside? no.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then original roads, but q1 is outside? no.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then original roads, but q1 is not broken because it's outside? no.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 wall, and q1 is at distance1, q2 at distance2. If an original road r is connected to q1 via q2 and then original roads, but q1 is already road? Then q1 not new; q2 new; r connected to q2 via q1? q1 original road, so r maybe in R_t? If q1 in R_t and r connected via q1, r in R_t. So not new. Good.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks q2 wall and q1 is original road in R_t. Then q2 new. Any new original road r connected to q2 via q1? q1 in R_t, so r would be in R_t. Thus no new r via q1. New r must be connected to q2 via original roads without q1? But q2 adjacent to q1 (road) and maybe other original roads. If r adjacent to q2, 0 edge q2->r. If r connected to q2 via q1, r in R_t. So static graph q2->r. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks q1 wall and q2 is original road in R_t? q2 could be road already reachable? If q2 road and q1 wall, after kick q1 broken, q2 remains road. New cells: q1. Any new original road r connected to q1 via q2? q2 in R_t, so r in R_t. So no new r via q2. New r adjacent to q1, 0 edge q1->r. Good.

Thus induction robust.

Now, let's consider if the graph with only walls as 1 edges might miss reaching a road cell that is at distance2 and distance1 is wall, but the distance1 wall is not added because it is already road? If distance1 is wall, added. If distance1 is road, then road cell at distance2 is reachable via 0 path if distance1 road. If distance1 road but not reachable? If distance1 road adjacent to current, it is reachable via 0 edge from current (since current road). So yes.

Now, let's think about if a kick can break a wall at distance2 even if distance1 is a wall that is also broken, but distance1 is not added because it's already broken? If already broken, no need for reaching distance1, but edge to distance2 still added. Good.

Now, let's consider if a kick can break a wall at distance2 even if distance1 is a wall that is also broken, and distance1 is not in R_t? It becomes broken by same kick, so edge to distance2 valid. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick can break a wall at distance2 even if distance1 is a wall that is also broken, and distance1 is outside? no.

Now, let's consider if a kick can break a wall at distance2 even if distance1 is a wall that is also broken, and distance1 is not inside? no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `S[x][y]` for current cell if current cell is wall and we never set it? We only pop cells whose dist set; if wall, it was set via 1 edge, so it is broken. We don't check S for current. Good.

Now, let's consider if a wall cell can be reached with dist d via a kick, but then 0 edge to adjacent original road, and from that road 0 edge to another original road, etc. Our code does that. Good.

Now, let's think about if a wall cell can be reached with dist d, and adjacent original road has already dist d (same), no update. Fine.

Now, let's consider if a wall cell can be reached with dist d, and adjacent original road has dist d+1 (larger), update to d. This is important: 0 edges from wall to road can improve road distance. Our code does that. Good.

Now, let's think about if an original road cell can be reached with dist d, and adjacent wall has dist d+1, but wall was broken by same kick that reached the road? Example kick breaks wall w and road r at distance2? Actually if road r at distance2 and wall w at distance1, after kick, both w and r reachable with cost d+1. Static graph: from current, edge to w cost d+1, then 0 edge to r cost d+1. It doesn't set r directly, but via w. If r is original road, it gets dist d+1. Good. If w already had dist d (lower), then r would have been reached from w earlier with dist d, so no issue. If w not added? w is wall, added. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks wall w at distance1 and wall v at distance2, and v is original wall. Static graph sets both w and v to d+1. From w, 0 edge to some road r. From v, 0 edge to some road s. Good.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks wall v at distance2 and distance1 is original road r. Static graph sets v to d+1. From v, 0 edge to r? r is original road adjacent to v, yes. But r may already have dist d (current) because current is adjacent? Wait current u, distance1 r, distance2 v. r is adjacent to u and original road, so r is reachable with dist d (maybe via 0 edge from u). From v (broken), 0 edge to r would set r to d+1, but r already d, no update. Fine. From r, 0 edge to v? v is wall, not 0. But v set d+1. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks wall v at distance2 and distance1 is original road r, and from v you need to move to r and then somewhere. Since r already reachable with d, no need. If you need to be at v then r then some road beyond r, r already reachable; you can go from r to beyond without v. So no issue.

Now, let's consider if a kick breaks wall w at distance1 and distance2 is original road r. Static graph sets w d+1, then 0 to r d+1. If r was already reachable with d? If r is distance2 from u and distance1 w wall, r not reachable before kick because w wall blocks? But r could be reachable via another path with d. If so, no issue. If not, set d+1. Good.

Now, let's think about if a kick breaks wall w at distance1 and distance2 is original road r, and from r you need to move to w (broken) and then somewhere. w set d+1, r set d+1. Static graph has r->w? w is wall, so 1 edge cost d+2, not 0. But if you need to go r->w->somewhere, w already reachable with d+1; any continuation from w is processed when w popped. If you need to do something at r first then w, r and w same dist; if action at r needed before going to w, then from r action breaks something; to then go to w, static graph might lack r->w 0. But if w reachable with same dist, you could do action at w first? Order might matter. Let's analyze this specific case: kick from u breaks w (dist1) and r (dist2 road). After kick, both w and r are road. Suppose optimal path: stand at r, perform a kick (say up) to break c, then move to w, then move to x (road adjacent to w). Total kicks: first kick + second kick = d+2? Actually d is dist before first kick. Static graph:
- u->w cost d+1.
- u->r? r is road, not set via 1 edge. But r can be reached via w->r 0 edge (r original road adjacent to w). So r dist d+1.
- From r, kick up to c cost d+2.
- Need reach x adjacent to w. From c, maybe not adjacent to x. Static graph lacks r->w 0 (w wall). But w has dist d+1. Target x adjacent to w would have been reached from w with dist d+1 when w popped, before the second kick. So if x is target, no need second kick. If target is beyond x and requires c? If c is needed to reach beyond x, then from c to beyond x path exists, no need w. If target requires both c and x? Target is single cell; if reachable from x, then x dist d+1, so target dist
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
from collections import deque

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline

    H, W = map(int, input().split())
    S = [input().strip() for _ in range(H)]

    A, B, C, D = map(int, input().split())
    A -= 1
    B -= 1
    C -= 1
    D -= 1

    start = A * W + B
    goal = C * W + D

    INF = 10**9
    dist = [INF] * (H * W)
    dist[start] = 0

    dq = deque([start])

    while dq:
        v = dq.popleft()
        d = dist[v]

        if v == goal:
            break

        x = v // W
        y = v - x * W

        # Move to adjacent original road: cost 0
        if x > 0:
            nv = v - W
            if S[x - 1][y] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)

        if x + 1 < H:
            nv = v + W
            if S[x + 1][y] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)

        if y > 0:
            nv = v - 1
            if S[x][y - 1] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)

        if y + 1 < W:
            nv = v + 1
            if S[x][y + 1] == '.' and dist[nv] > d:
                dist[nv] = d
                dq.appendleft(nv)

        nd = d + 1

        # Front kick: walls at distance 1 and 2 become roads: cost 1
        # Up
        if x >= 2:
            nv = v - 2 * W
            if S[x - 2][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if x >= 1:
            nv = v - W
            if S[x - 1][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)

        # Down
        if x + 2 < H:
            nv = v + 2 * W
            if S[x + 2][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if x + 1 < H:
            nv = v + W
            if S[x + 1][y] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)

        # Left
        if y >= 2:
            nv = v - 2
            if S[x][y - 2] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if y >= 1:
            nv = v - 1
            if S[x][y - 1] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)

        # Right
        if y + 2 < W:
            nv = v + 2
            if S[x][y + 2] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)
        if y + 1 < W:
            nv = v + 1
            if S[x][y + 1] == '#' and dist[nv] > nd:
                dist[nv] = nd
                dq.append(nv)

    print(dist[goal])

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
